- Consolidated data - Different aspects of governance -
Aspect Description Key Features Examples/Applications
Management Overseeing and directing organizational or institutional operations to achieve goals. Strategic planning, resource allocation, leadership, decision-making processes. Corporate board of directors setting company policies; Government ministries implementing national development plans (e.g., India’s NITI Aayog).
Audits Systematic review of financial, operational, or compliance processes to ensure accountability. Independent evaluation, risk assessment, transparency, regulatory compliance. Comptroller and Auditor General (CAG) of India auditing public sector accounts; ISO 9001 audits for quality management systems.
Participation Engagement of stakeholders in decision-making to enhance inclusivity and legitimacy. Public consultations, participatory democracy, stakeholder forums, inclusive policy-making. Gram Sabha meetings in India for village-level governance; UN’s public consultations for SDG implementation.
Transparency Ensuring openness in processes, decisions, and information sharing to build trust. Open data policies, public disclosure, accessible records, accountability mechanisms. Right to Information (RTI) Act in India; EU’s transparency register for lobbying activities.
Accountability Holding individuals or organizations responsible for their actions and performance. Performance metrics, grievance redressal, legal frameworks, oversight bodies. Lokpal in India addressing public sector corruption; UN Accountability Framework for peacekeeping operations.
Policy Development Creating frameworks and guidelines to address societal or organizational needs. Evidence-based policy, stakeholder input, legislative alignment, monitoring mechanisms. India’s National Education Policy (NEP) 2020; UNFCCC’s Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) for climate action.
Risk Management Identifying, assessing, and mitigating risks to ensure organizational stability. Risk assessment frameworks, contingency planning, internal controls, crisis management. Basel III norms for banking risk management; India’s Disaster Management Act, 2005, for natural calamities.

ARC Report/Recommendation Report Number/Year Focus Area Purpose/Description Key Steps/Models/Recommendations
Citizen-Centric Administration
Citizen's Charter 12th Report (2009) Transparency & Service Delivery - Objective: Voluntary framework to outline services, standards, and grievance mechanisms for accountable public service delivery.
- Provisions: Details service entitlements, timelines, and redressal processes; promotes citizen trust.
- Application: Adopted in 1997; used by ministries (e.g., Railways, Passports) and PSUs. 		Components: (1) Vision/Mission; (2) Service list; (3) Delivery timelines; (4) Grievance redressal; (5) Citizen expectations; (6) Contact details. Steps: (1) Stakeholder consultation; (2) Draft charter; (3) Publish; (4) Train staff; (5) Review biannually; (6) Publicize; (7) Link to appraisals. Recommendations: Mandatory for all public entities; integrate with Sevottam; awareness campaigns.
Sevottam Model 12th Report (2009) Service Excellence - Objective: Ensure excellence in public service delivery via quality management (aligned with IS 15700:2005).
- Provisions: Three modules: Citizen Charter, grievance redressal, capability enhancement.
- Application: Benchmarking for ministries; BIS certification for compliance. 		Modules: (1) Charter with measurable standards; (2) Effective grievance system; (3) Capacity building. Seven-Step Model: (1) Assess delivery status; (2) Set citizen-centric goals; (3) Map processes; (4) Identify gaps; (5) Implement reforms; (6) Monitor KPIs; (7) Feedback loop. Recommendations: Mandatory adoption; annual audits; incentives for certified entities.
Grievance Redressal Mechanisms 12th Report (2009) Accountability - Objective: Timely resolution of citizen complaints to enhance responsiveness.
- Provisions: CPGRAMS integration; time-bound processes; multi-channel access.
- Application: Central/state ministries, field offices; linked to charters. 		Steps: (1) Multi-channel complaint receipt; (2) Acknowledge within 3 days; (3) Categorize/assign; (4) Resolve in 30-60 days; (5) Citizen feedback; (6) Analyze trends; (7) Preventive actions. Recommendations: Dedicated nodal officers; AI-based tracking; escalation protocols; public resolution data.
Right to Information & Transparency
Right to Information Act Implementation 1st Report (2006) Transparency - Objective: Promote openness via effective RTI Act (2005) implementation.
- Provisions: Proactive disclosure (Section 4); Public Information Officers (PIOs); appeals.
- Application: All public authorities; empowers citizens to access information. 		Steps: (1) Appoint PIOs; (2) Train officials; (3) Publish Section 4 info; (4) Process requests in 30 days; (5) Appellate mechanism; (6) Monitor compliance; (7) Annual CIC reporting. Recommendations: Repeal Official Secrets Act 1923; mandatory training; e-filing; reduce backlog.
Proactive Disclosure 1st Report (2006) Transparency - Objective: Minimize RTI requests through suo motu disclosure.
- Provisions: Section 4 mandates publication of budgets, policies, decisions.
- Application: Websites/portals of ministries, PSUs. 		Steps: (1) Identify disclosable data; (2) Publish online; (3) Update quarterly; (4) Media dissemination; (5) Train staff; (6) Monitor compliance; (7) Citizen feedback. Recommendations: 80% disclosure target; link to appraisals; penalize non-compliance.
Unlocking Human Capital - RTI Rules 2nd Report (2006) Transparency - Objective: Strengthen RTI for scheme monitoring (e.g., NREGA).
- Provisions: Social audits; community-level disclosure.
- Application: Rural development schemes, public works. 		Steps: (1) Conduct social audits; (2) Publish scheme details; (3) Train local bodies; (4) Monitor via RTI; (5) Feedback integration; (6) Public hearings; (7) Corrective actions. Recommendations: Mandatory social audits; RTI cells in districts; public access points.
Ethics & Accountability
Ethics in Governance 4th Report (2007) Ethics - Objective: Promote integrity, reduce corruption in public service.
- Provisions: Code of conduct; anti-corruption measures.
- Application: Public servants, ministers, judiciary. 		Steps for Code: (1) Define values (e.g., integrity, impartiality); (2) Training; (3) Monitor adherence; (4) Enforce via sanctions; (5) Annual review. Recommendations: Comprehensive Code of Ethics; Integrity Pacts; Lokpal; judicial standards; transparent political funding; asset declarations.
Lokpal & Lokayuktas 4th Report (2007) Accountability - Objective: Independent ombudsman to investigate corruption.
- Provisions: Covers ministers, MPs; independent investigation wing.
- Application: Central (Lokpal), state (Lokayukta). 		Steps: (1) Appoint via PM/LoP/CJI committee; (2) 5-year term; (3) Independent staff; (4) Inquiries within 6 months; (5) Annual reports; (6) Public disclosure; (7) Coordinate with CVC. Recommendations: No political interference; judicial coverage; strong enforcement.
Whistleblower Protection 4th Report (2007) Accountability - Objective: Protect whistleblowers exposing corruption.
- Provisions: Anonymity, legal safeguards; Whistleblowers Protection Act 2014.
- Application: Public/private sector whistleblowers. 		Steps: (1) Establish reporting channels; (2) Ensure anonymity; (3) Legal protection; (4) Fast-track investigations; (5) Monitor outcomes; (6) Publicize protections; (7) Train officials. Recommendations: Amend 2014 Act for private sector; nodal agency; incentives.
E-Governance & Capacity Building
E-Governance for Good Governance 11th Report (2008) Efficiency - Objective: Use ICT for transparent, efficient administration.
- Provisions: National e-Governance Plan (NeGP); digital service delivery.
- Application: Grievances, service delivery (e.g., Aadhaar, DBT). 		Steps: (1) Build IT infrastructure; (2) Re-engineer processes; (3) Train officials; (4) Deploy Common Service Centres; (5) Ensure cybersecurity; (6) Monitor via dashboards; (7) Citizen feedback. Recommendations: Expand NeGP; open standards; digital charters; 100% online services.
Capacity Building & Mission Karmayogi 10th Report (2008) Capacity - Objective: Continuous training for civil servants.
- Provisions: iGOT platform; behavioral, functional competencies.
- Application: 2M+ employees; ethics and governance training. 		Steps: (1) Assess training needs; (2) Design curriculum; (3) Deliver via e-learning; (4) Assess performance; (5) Certify; (6) Link to promotions; (7) Annual refreshers. Recommendations: Lateral entry; 10M+ training hours; public-private training partnerships.
Local Governance
Local Governance Reforms 6th Report (2007) Decentralization - Objective: Empower PRIs/ULBs for effective self-governance.
- Provisions: Activity mapping; fund devolution; 73rd/74th Amendments.
- Application: Panchayats, municipalities across states. 		Steps: (1) Map activities; (2) Devolve funds/functions; (3) Train elected representatives; (4) Establish audit committees; (5) Strengthen Gram Sabhas; (6) Monitor performance; (7) Allocate performance grants. Recommendations: District Councils; State Finance Commissions; e-Gram Swaraj; 50% women reservations.
Social Capital 9th Report (2008) Community Governance - Objective: Leverage NGOs, SHGs, cooperatives for governance.
- Provisions: Partnerships with civil society; support for SHGs.
- Application: Rural development, women empowerment. 		Steps: (1) Map NGOs/SHGs; (2) Fund via schemes; (3) Train community leaders; (4) Monitor projects; (5) Integrate with PRIs; (6) Evaluate impact; (7) Scale successful models. Recommendations: NGO DARPAN mandatory; fund SHGs; incentivize cooperatives; publicize best practices.
Crisis & Conflict Management
Crisis Management 3rd Report (2006) Disaster Management - Objective: Strengthen disaster preparedness and response.
- Provisions: NDMA guidelines; state/district plans.
- Application: Natural disasters (floods, earthquakes), man-made crises. 		Steps: (1) Risk mapping; (2) Prepare plans; (3) Train responders; (4) Build infrastructure; (5) Conduct drills; (6) Coordinate agencies; (7) Post-disaster evaluation. Recommendations: Strengthen NDMA; community-based DRR; early warning systems; PPPs for relief.
Public Order 5th Report (2007) Security - Objective: Enhance law and order management.
- Provisions: Police reforms, community policing.
- Application: Riots, public unrest, internal security. 		Steps: (1) Modernize police; (2) Train for community policing; (3) Enhance intelligence; (4) Coordinate agencies; (5) Public engagement; (6) Monitor outcomes; (7) Implement SC directives. Recommendations: Police Act 2007; separate law/order wings; NDRF expansion.
Combating Terrorism 8th Report (2008) Security - Objective: Strengthen counter-terrorism framework.
- Provisions: NIA establishment; intelligence coordination.
- Application: Terrorism, insurgency (e.g., Naxalism). 		Steps: (1) Establish NIA; (2) Share intelligence; (3) Train forces; (4) Strengthen borders; (5) Enforce UAPA; (6) Engage communities; (7) International cooperation. Recommendations: Integrated agency; cyber security; fast-track courts; community deradicalization.
Conflict Resolution 8th Report (2008) Security - Objective: Address regional conflicts (e.g., Northeast, Naxalism).
- Provisions: Dialogue, development, security measures.
- Application: J&K, Northeast, Red Corridor. 		Steps: (1) Map conflict zones; (2) Engage stakeholders; (3) Deploy security; (4) Fund development; (5) Monitor peace talks; (6) Rehabilitate affected; (7) Evaluate outcomes. Recommendations: Special status review; development packages; autonomous councils.
Financial & Administrative Reforms
Strengthening Financial Management 7th Report (2008) Finance - Objective: Improve public financial management.
- Provisions: Outcome budgeting, internal audits.
- Application: Union/state budgets, public expenditure. 		Steps: (1) Adopt outcome budgeting; (2) Conduct internal audits; (3) Implement e-procurement; (4) Train accountants; (5) Monitor expenditure; (6) CAG oversight; (7) Public reporting. Recommendations: Independent fiscal council; performance audits; PPP guidelines.
Financial Management Systems 14th Report (2009) Finance - Objective: Enhance efficiency in public spending.
- Provisions: PFMS, accrual accounting.
- Application: Central schemes, state finances. 		Steps: (1) Implement PFMS; (2) Shift to accrual accounting; (3) Train finance staff; (4) Integrate budgets; (5) Monitor real-time; (6) Audit regularly; (7) Publish outcomes. Recommendations: Full accrual accounting by 2015; PFMS for all schemes; CAG empowerment.
State and District Administration 15th Report (2009) Administration - Objective: Strengthen district-level governance.
- Provisions: Decentralized planning, collector empowerment.
- Application: District administration, field offices. 		Steps: (1) Empower collectors; (2) Decentralize planning; (3) Train officials; (4) Integrate PRIs; (5) Monitor performance; (6) Citizen feedback; (7) Adopt e-governance. Recommendations: District as governance unit; performance-based transfers; citizen report cards.
Personnel Administration 10th Report (2008) Administration - Objective: Reform civil services for efficiency.
- Provisions: Recruitment, performance appraisal reforms.
- Application: IAS, IPS, other services. 		Steps: (1) Revamp UPSC selection; (2) Introduce 360-degree appraisals; (3) Train mid-career; (4) Allow lateral entry; (5) Monitor performance; (6) Link promotions; (7) Exit underperformers. Recommendations: Domain specialization; fixed tenure; merit-based promotions.
Judicial & Legal Reforms
Judicial Reforms 4th Report (2007) Judiciary - Objective: Enhance judicial efficiency, reduce pendency.
- Provisions: Fast-track courts, judicial standards.
- Application: All courts, tribunals. 		Steps: (1) Increase judges; (2) Establish fast-track courts; (3) Adopt case management; (4) Train judges; (5) Monitor pendency; (6) Use e-courts; (7) Public reporting. Recommendations: National Judicial Council; alternative dispute resolution; reduce backlog to 5 years.
Legal Education and Profession 4th Report (2007) Legal System - Objective: Improve legal education and bar standards.
- Provisions: Bar Council reforms, curriculum overhaul.
- Application: Law schools, advocates. 		Steps: (1) Revamp curriculum; (2) Introduce bar exams; (3) Train advocates; (4) Monitor ethics; (5) Accredit institutions; (6) Publicize standards; (7) Reform BCI. Recommendations: National Law University model; mandatory internships; ethical training.
- Current affairs -
- Chapter 1 - IMPORTANT ASPECTS OF GOVERNANCE - 1. Introduction - Government performs a variety of functions: - - Governance builds upon these functions by encompassing the broader processes, mechanisms, and interactions through which these governmental responsibilities are executed. - - A key distinction between government and governance is the involvement of institutions beyond the state in managing public affairs. While the state wields power and is the principal actor in government, governance involves the entire society in managing public affairs. - 2. Governance - Governance is a complex web of decision-making processes that involves various stakeholders, including government bodies, non-governmental organizations, local leaders, and citizens. It refers to the way decisions, rules, and actions are made and implemented to effectively manage a country or organization’s affairs. - 2.1. Good Governance - Good governance means managing and running an organisation or country in a fair, transparent, and effective way, ensuring that the needs and interests of the people are met. - The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP), 1997, defined governance as “the exercise of economic, political and administrative authority to manage a country’s affairs at all levels. It comprises the mechanisms, processes and institutions, through which citizens and groups articulate their interests, exercise their legal rights, meet their obligations and mediate their differences.” - Good governance is considered key to achieving sustainable development and human well-being. Empirical studies show that good governance, in contrast to democratization, has strong positive effects on measures of social trust, life satisfaction, peace and political legitimacy. - UNDP recognizes eight core characteristics of good governance as mentioned in the table below: -
PrincipleDetailsExamples
ParticipationActive involvement of all societal sectors in decision-making, enabled by timely information and free expression.Public forums; civil society engagement in policy-making.
Rule of LawState power guided by published, widely supported social values, with laws applying equally to all, including the state itself.Independent judiciary; constitutional limits on executive power; laws consistent with human rights.
TransparencyDecision-making processes are open to scrutiny by society, with officials providing information and people having access to it.Free press; publicly accessible government procedures and data.
ResponsivenessInstitutions promptly and appropriately serve all stakeholders, protecting citizens' interests and addressing discrimination.Citizen's charters with service timelines; mechanisms for grievance redressal.
Consensus OrientationSystems aim for the best interests of society by considering diverse viewpoints to find common ground or compromise.Public hearings; referendums; mediation structures for policy disputes.
Equity & InclusivenessAll individuals, especially the vulnerable, have opportunities for well-being and are considered in policymaking.Progressive taxation; subsidized essential services; policies benefiting the least advantaged.
Government Efficiency & EffectivenessInstitutions make optimal use of resources to deliver results meeting societal needs, with quality services and minimal waste.Streamlined public services; professional bureaucracy; performance-based management.
AccountabilityIndividuals and institutions are answerable for their actions, especially concerning the public interest.Checks and balances within government; citizens' ability to demand explanations; codes of conduct for officials.
Note: Many sources include “Strategic Vision” also as a 9th principle of Good Governance. A strategic vision refers to a clear, long-term plan that guides how a government aims to improve and manage its services and responsibilities.
- - - Student Notes: - Virtuous Cycle of Good Governance - - A "virtuous cycle of good governance" describes a positive feedback loop where effective governance leads to increased legitimacy, which in turn fosters greater effectiveness and citizen trust. This cycle promotes development, strengthens democracy, and improves the overall well-being of society. - However, the conversion of effective governance into legitimacy depends on several conditions: shared values, correct attribution of effectiveness, and governance actors’ capacity to transform legitimacy into impactful policies. - 2.1.1. Pre-conditions & Tools for Good Governance - - The 2nd ARC identified several preconditions which must be fulfilled in order to make governance good and citizen centric. - Having established this foundational knowledge of good governance, we will proceed to have a look at governance in India, followed by a detailed discussion of few key tools of good governance, such as social audit, citizens' charter, and e-governance. - - 3. Governance in India - Governance in India has evolved through distinct phases, from British colonial rule focused on consolidating power to post-independence nation-building emphasizing social justice and economic development. Post 1991 economic reforms that introduced a market-driven economy, developments such as decentralization, digitization etc. have led to greater transparency, increased citizen engagement, enhanced accessibility and efficiency of governance. - 3.1. Various Dimensions of Governance - The Department of Administrative Reforms and Public Grievances (DARPG) in its report “State of Governance – A framework of assessment” has broken down governance into five dimensions, viz., political, legal & judicial, administrative, economic and social & environmental dimensions. - - 3.2. Governance Issues in India - India faces a range of governance related issues in political, economic, administrative, social and legal domain. Some factors attributable to poor governance are: - - 3.3. Barriers to Good Governance - Barriers to good governance arise from the attitudes and actions of some government officials, weaknesses in institutional structures, and citizen-related factors. Although laws may be well-designed, poor implementation and insufficient institutional capacity often hinder their effective enforcement. - - Student Notes: - Ethics in Governance - Ethics is a set of standards that society places on itself and which helps guide behaviors, choices and actions. Corruption and the abuse of office is an important manifestation of the failure of ethics. Corruption and good governance have a two-way causal relationship with each other and feed off each other in a vicious circle. - - 3.4. Good Governance Initiatives in India - - Good governance is an ongoing process aiming to serve the people better—this commitment to service underpins the core philosophy of a democratic government. To foster good governance, GoI has launched various initiatives: - - Minimum Government, Maximum Governance (MGMG): - Does shrinking government automatically leads to better governance? - MGMG is a slogan that suggests limiting government size while enhancing its efficiency and accountability. It focuses on streamlined government structure that minimizes bureaucratic red tape. - While developed countries like Scandinavia have large governments and strong economies, Asian nations generally have smaller governments. India’s government size has grown modestly compared to its GDP. - In developing countries, the government plays an important role in providing public services and infrastructure. Focus should be on smart governance, strategic public expenditure, and placing the right people in positions. - Letting Go is Part of Good Governance - The Economic Survey 2023-24 emphasizes that letting go is crucial for good governance. As the world faces potential conflicts and challenges, the Indian state must focus on critical areas by reducing its grip on non-essential sectors. - 4. Important Tools to Implement Good Governance (in Detail) - The implementation of good governance can be achieved through various tools and mechanisms, including people’s participation, the Right to Information (RTI), the Sevottam model, Citizen Charters, and e-governance. - 4.1. Enhancing People’s Participation - People’s participation is a vital component of good governance, reflecting the principle that governance should be for the people, by the people, and with the people. - Licensing, inspection, and compliance requirements continue to burden businesses, particularly small and medium enterprises. This excessive regulation stifles their growth and holds the country back. - Active involvement of citizens in the decision-making process ensures that governance is not only representative but also responsive to the needs and aspirations of the community. - This participatory approach enhances the legitimacy, accountability, and effectiveness of governance. - - Benefits of People’s Participation - 1. Enhanced Legitimacy: - When citizens are actively involved in the governance process, decisions made are more likely to be accepted and respected, thereby enhancing the legitimacy of the government. - 2. Improved Accountability: - Participation increases transparency and accountability as citizens can hold their representatives and public officials accountable for their actions and decisions. - 3. Better Decision-Making: - Incorporating diverse perspectives leads to more comprehensive and informed decision-making, ensuring that policies and programs are more effective and inclusive. - 4. Empowerment: - Participation empowers citizens by giving them a voice in governance and enabling them to influence decisions that affect their lives. - This fosters a sense of ownership and responsibility towards community development. - 5. Social Cohesion: - Active participation promotes social cohesion by bringing together diverse groups to work towards common goals, fostering a sense of unity and collective effort. - Challenges to People’s Participation - 1. Lack of Awareness: - Many citizens are unaware of their rights and the mechanisms available for participation, which limits their engagement in the governance process. - 2. Socio-Economic Barriers: - Socio-economic disparities can hinder participation, as marginalized and disadvantaged groups may lack the resources and opportunities to engage effectively. - 3. Institutional Constraints: - Bureaucratic red tape, lack of responsiveness, and inadequate infrastructure can pose significant barriers to effective citizen participation. - 4. Political Will: - Genuine participation requires political will and commitment from those in power to create and maintain inclusive and participatory governance mechanisms. - Student Notes: - Seven Sins of People’s Participation - Andrea Cornwall’s "Seven Sins of Citizen Participation" highlight pitfalls where engagement is superficial, not genuine. - These manifest across various development and governance initiatives: - 1. Cosmetic Labeling- Participation as an illusion - Example: - Environmental Public Hearings (EPHs) for projects, often function as mere procedural formalities, with public concerns frequently ignored in final decisions. - 2. Paternalism- Assuming "We Know Best." - Example: - Top-down development schemes like some urban slum redevelopment projects in cities like Mumbai or Delhi impose solutions, disregarding community needs and traditional wisdom, leading to poor adoption. - 3. Politicization- Participation as a political tool - Example: - The functioning of Gram Sabhas can be manipulated by local power politics, influencing outcomes and resource allocation to favor dominant groups, as sometimes observed in social audits under MGNREGA. - 4. Poor Representation- Marginalizing voices - Example: - Despite constitutional reservations, women and marginalized groups in Panchayati Raj Institutions often struggle to exert influence, a phenomenon sometimes termed "Sarpanch Pati." - 5. Instrumentalization- Participation as a means to an end - Example: - Some NGOs organize community consultations primarily to meet funding checklist requirements for watershed development projects, rather than for genuine input. - 6. Fragmentation- Uncoordinated efforts, diluted impact - Example: - In urban areas, multiple government departments (e.g., for water and sanitation) often initiate separate "participatory" consultations without adequate coordination, leading to confusion and ineffective outcomes. - 7. Overloading- Participation fatigue and disengagement - Example: - ASHA workers face immense workloads and numerous responsibilities across health programs, leading to burnout and hindering their capacity for sustained community engagement. - Addressing these sins requires a fundamental shift towards genuine inclusivity, transparency, and empowering citizens as active agents in their own development and governance. - People’s participation is indispensable for achieving good governance. It ensures that governance is not only representative but also responsive, transparent, and accountable. A participatory approach to governance strengthens democracy and promotes sustainable development. - 4.2. Sevottam Model - - The Sevottam model has been developed with objective of improving the quality of public service delivery in the country. It is a combination of two words Seva (service) and Uttam (excellence). - It provides a framework for organizations to assess and improve the quality-of-service delivery to citizens. The model was suggested by 2nd ARC in its 12th report Citizen Centric administration. - The model has three modules: - Citizen Charter - Public Grievance Redress mechanism - Service Delivery Capability - - Organizations adopting Sevottam model for service delivery need to comply with seven steps: - 1. Define services and identify clients. - 2. Set standards and norms for each service. - 3. Develop capability to meet the set standards. - 4. Perform to achieve the standards. - 5. Monitor performance against the set standards. - 6. Evaluate impact through an independent mechanism. - 7. Continuous improvement based on monitoring and evaluation - Department of Post (India Post) - Implementation of Sevottam framework in the government departments was started in 2009. - Significance of the Model - It guides them through systematic initiatives for sustainable improvements in service delivery. - The framework enables implementing organizations to undertake a systematic, credible and authenticated self-assessment (or ‘gap analysis’) for citizen-centric service delivery. - Using this analysis, practical solutions are gradually and systematically incorporated into the organization’s day-to-day routine thereby ensuring sustainable results. - Some success stories of Sevottam framework - India Post, with its vast network of post offices, embraced Sevottam. - They improved services like speed post, parcel delivery, and savings accounts. - Indian Railways: - The lifeline of India, Indian Railways, implemented Sevottam across various services—ticket booking, cleanliness, and passenger amenities. - Passport Offices: - Sevottam transformed passport services. - Citizens now encounter streamlined processes, faster issuance, and improved customer support. - 4.2.1. Citizen Charter - A Citizens’ Charter is basically a set of commitments made by an organization regarding the standards of service which it delivers (2nd ARC). - It is a public statement that defines the entitlements of citizens to a specific service, the standards of the service, the conditions to be met by users, and the remedies available to the latter in case of non-compliance of standards. - Principles of Citizen Charter - Originally, six principles of the Citizens Charter movement are framed. (Refer infographic) - - Significance of Citizen Charter - It is a tool to achieve good governance: - It provides services to the people in a time bound manner, redressing their grievances and improving their lives. - It enshrines the trust between the service provider and its users and empowers the citizen in relation to public service delivery. - - Citizen Charter in India - The concept was first articulated and implemented in the United Kingdom in 1991. India adopted citizen’s charter in 1997. - The Department of Administrative Reforms and Public Grievances (DARPG) initiated the task of coordinating, formulating and operationalising Citizen's Charters. - The Right of Citizens for Time Bound Delivery of Goods and Services and Redressal of their Grievances Bill, 2011 (Citizens Charter) seeks to create a mechanism to ensure timely delivery of goods and services to citizens. However, it lapsed due to the dissolution of the Lok Sabha in 2014. - Challenges in Implementing Citizens Charter - - Strengthening Citizen Charters - - 4.3. Social Accountability - Social accountability refers to a wide range of actions and mechanisms that the citizens, communities, independent media and civil society organizations can use to hold public officials accountable. - - Open Government - OECD defines open government as - the transparency of government actions, - the accessibility of government services and information, and - the responsiveness of government to new ideas, demands and needs. - Common Tools of Social Accountability at Various Stages of Service Delivery Involves the Following: - - Challenges and Vulnerabilities Inherent to Social Accountability Efforts - Lack of Transparency: - Withholding crucial information or providing inadequate information, such as budget documents, necessary for social accountability initiatives. - Complacency on Part of Citizens: - Citizens not speaking up against corruption and refusing to cooperate in social accountability efforts. - Disruption by Powerful Vested Interests: - Threats and coercion result in communities becoming hesitant to participate in social accountability initiatives. - Lack of Effective Grievance Redressal: - Strict and unfailing follow-up action on social accountability findings is lacking. - - Key Elements of Effective Social Accountability - Effective Decentralization: - Empower local governments, such as panchayats and municipalities, by providing them with the necessary resources and authority to implement schemes and programs based on the "principle of subsidiarity." - Information and Awareness: - Enhance Section 4 of the RTI Act to promote the proactive disclosure of relevant and accurate information. - Capacity Building and Community Mobilization: - Regularly train officials and citizens, and involve local NGOs, SHGs, and community groups to increase awareness and encourage participation in schemes. - Mandatory Social Accountability: - Require social audits in programs with significant public impact to ensure accountability. - Student Notes: - Supply-side Accountability and Demand-side Accountability: -

- 3.3.1. Social Audit - Social audit is a process where a public agency shares details of resources used in development initiatives with the public, often through public platforms. This process allows end users to scrutinize the effectiveness of developmental programs. - Social audit serves as an instrument for the measurement of social accountability of an organization. - - 3.3.1.1. Significance of Social Audit - Social audits empower citizens to actively monitor programs, ensuring transparency and better outcomes. Below are the key benefits of social audits: - 1. Boosts Accountability and Transparency - Social audits enhance both supply-side and demand-side accountability by scrutinizing fund utilization and program implementation. Public hearings (jan sunwais) and community-led reviews expose discrepancies, ensuring corrective action. For instance: - In Rajasthan, social audits under NREGA uncovered fraud in wage payments and labor records, leading to fund recovery and stricter oversight. - By making government data publicly accessible, social audits allow collective interrogation, reducing misuse of resources. - 2. Strengthens Governance and Community Engagement - While supply-side accountability relies on administrative checks, social audits strengthen demand-side accountability by empowering local communities through platforms like Gram Sabhas. Marginalized groups, especially women, gain a voice in monitoring entitlements and demanding transparency. - 3. Enhances Program Design and Delivery - Social audits bridge gaps between policy goals and ground realities, providing feedback to improve supply-side systems. - For example: - In Odisha, audits across 240 Gram Panchayats improved nutrition service delivery by identifying implementation flaws. - States like Andhra Pradesh and Telangana scaled up audits, leading to better NREGA performance and expanded oversight in food security and juvenile justice. - 4. Empowers Marginalized Groups - By involving disadvantaged communities in monitoring, social audits ensure supply-side accountability is complemented by grassroots participation. Women and marginalized groups can challenge inefficiencies, ensuring equitable service delivery. - 5. Delivers Tangible Improvements - Observable outcomes include: - Better public service delivery (e.g., schools opening on time, improved teacher attendance). - Efficient grievance redressal through structured feedback mechanisms. - Policy reforms aligning programs with community needs, strengthening both supply and demand-side accountability. - Student Notes: - CASE STUDY (2021) - This study transformed the social audit format for MGNREGA into a social audit of education, and drew the following stakeholder perspectives on the effectiveness of social audits: -
StakeholderPerspectives
Community MembersIn Rajasthan, communities recalled positive impacts on teacher punctuality and school timings.
In Delhi, initial community distrust was noted due to a lack of prior mobilization and skepticism about community-based organizations' effectiveness.
Government School TeachersExperienced increased accountability, leading to more punctual school operations.
Faced interference from vested interests, including local Panchayat officials, who often sided with teachers.

Frontline auditors faced obstacles due to uneven responses from state governments to audit findings (e.g., in Telangana).
Social Audit Team (Activists/Facilitators)Found the process challenging due to resistance but gained respect.
Played a crucial role in accessing and translating government information and documenting user testimonies.
- The study concluded that building bridges between stakeholders and state actors, and working on the basis of collaboration rather than confrontation, play an important role in making social audits work. - 3.3.1.2. Limitations of Social Audit - Despite their importance, social audits come with several limitations that can hinder their comprehensive application and effectiveness: - Localized Scope: - Social audits are highly localized, focusing only on selected aspects and not broadly applicable across different regions or the entire population. - It’s findings are context-specific and not universally applicable, as they reflect unique local conditions and stakeholder feedback specific to the area audited. - Inconsistency and Ad Hoc Nature: - These audits often occur sporadically and lack a formal, continuous monitoring process. - Not Suitable for All Programs: - Each program faces unique challenges, and social audits may not always be effective. For instance, adult literacy programs for migrants need migration data, which might not be covered in a typical social audit. - Lack of Trained Auditors: - There is a notable shortage of professionals trained specifically to conduct effective social audits as audit require a distinct set of skills and knowledge. - Inaction on Audit Findings: - Often, there is no subsequent action taken on the findings and recommendations of social audits, limiting their effectiveness and impact. - Improving auditor training, broadening the audits' scope, and ensuring actions are taken based on findings are vital for enhancing their effectiveness. These improvements can make social audits stronger tools for community development and governance. - 3.3.1.3. Recommendations for Strengthening Social Audit in India - - These comprehensive steps aim to revitalize the social audit mechanism in India, ensuring it not only checks compliance but also drives meaningful improvements in governance and public trust. - Social Audits Supplementing CAG Audits (CAG+1) - Social audits can significantly enhance CAG audits by providing an outcome orientation that broadens the scope—referred to as CAG+1—focusing on performance outputs, and supplying vital data, reports, and findings. - The CAG has directed all state Auditors General (AGs) to support the institutionalization of social audits. - CAG has emphasized the importance of AGs assisting in strengthening social audits by providing inputs, advising social audit teams in gram sabhas, panchayats, and NGOs on report making, data collection, and ensuring policy implementation. - Proposed dedicated portal at state level to manage all social audits, making it accessible to the CAG. - This integrated approach, called CAG+1, is expected to significantly strengthen the auditing system. - 4.4. E-Governance - What is E-governance? - According to the World Bank, “E-Governance refers to the use by government agencies of information technologies (such as Wide Area Networks, the Internet, and mobile computing) that have the ability to transform relations with citizens, businesses, and other arms of government”. - The adoption of technology in governance facilitates more effective communication, informed decision-making and streamlined workflows. The framework of e-governance is often described through the "5C" model, as identified by Miglani in 2001, which includes: - Computer - Encompasses all necessary hardware and software for government functions. - Connectivity - Relates to systems that handle data transmission, including bandwidth and other information carriers. - Content - Refers to the information exchanged within the system among users. - Consumer - Includes all individuals and their proxies who access and utilize this content. - Confidence Building - Consists of measures aimed at enhancing citizen trust in the e-governance system, encouraging their engagement in e-transformation. - Potential of E-governance - - Fast, Convenient and Cost-Effective Service Delivery: - With the advent of e-Service delivery, the government can provide information and services at lesser costs, in reduced time and with greater convenience. - Transparency, Accountability and Reduced Corruption: - Dissemination of information improves the awareness levels of citizens about their rights and powers. It helps to reduce the discretionary powers of government officials and curtail corruption. - Expanded Reach of Governance: - Expansion of telephone network, spread of internet and strengthening of other communications infrastructure facilitated delivery of a number of public services. - Empowering People Through Information: - Increased accessibility to information has empowered the citizens and has enhanced their participation. - Improve Interface with Business and Industry: - Industrial development in India has been hampered in the past with complex procedures and bureaucratic delays. E-governance aims to expedite the various processes important for industrial development. - - Ease of Governance - As per this idea, the main aim of e-governance should be ‘ease of governance’ leading to ‘ease of living’ of people. This is essential for the emergence of a ‘New India’. - Ease of Governance essentially means Ease of access to governance where public policies are people centric with empathetic and responsive government mechanisms. - E-governance and JAM Trinity - The JAM Trinity, comprising Jan Dhan, Aadhaar, and Mobile connectivity, has revolutionized governance in India. Jan Dhan facilitates financial inclusion and direct benefits transfer, reducing leakages. Aadhaar provides unique IDs to authenticate identities and ensure benefits reach the right people. Mobile connectivity enables digital transactions and improves service delivery. - “For every rupee targeted towards welfare and poverty alleviation only a fraction, 15 paise, reached the intended beneficiary.” Rajiv Gandhi (1980s). - - “The amount that reaches the poor has reached 50%.” Montek Singh Ahluwalia (2007). - This implementation of JAM trinity enhances efficiency and transparency. Direct Benefits Transfer (DBT) through Jan Dhan accounts reduces corruption and ensures timely subsidies. Aadhaar minimizes fraud, while mobile connectivity streamlines processes for easier access to services. - 4.4.1. Models of E-governance - There are various models for e-governance, each with distinct strength and usefulness. - 1. Broadcasting Model - Key Features: - One-way dissemination of information from government to citizens. It’s a unidirectional communication model. - Usefulness: - Ideal for general public information (e.g., policy updates, public service announcements). E.g., Use in emergency alerts or public announcements where citizen feedback is unnecessary. - Examples: - Government websites, TV channels like Doordarshan, social media announcements. - Limitations: - No feedback loop—citizens cannot interact. - 2. Critical Flow Model - Key Features: - Targeted communication to specific groups with priority-based distribution. The information is relevant and customized for the audience. - Usefulness: - Useful when communicating critical, timely data to specific stakeholder groups (e.g., healthcare professionals, pensioners, businesses). - Examples: - Emergency alerts, pension scheme updates, medical insurance details for doctors. - Advantage: - Focused and prioritized distribution of information. - 3. Comparative Analysis Model - Key Features: - In this model, benchmarks are created and regional parameters are compared to it. - Usefulness: - Good for performance evaluations and benchmarking and comparison across jurisdictions. - Examples: - Dashboards comparing government performance, educational outcomes, health indicators. - Advantages: - Emphasizes performance metrics and data visualization for comparison. - 4. E-Advocacy/Mobilization and Lobbying Model - Key Features: - This model creates online forums to gather public opinion on policies, fostering diverse virtual communities that share ideas, expertise, and resources through digital networking. - Usefulness: - Promotes public participation and engagement and policy change through citizen-driven initiatives. Useful for issues requiring citizen mobilization and collective action. - Examples: - Online petitions, social media campaigns, direct interactions with government decision-makers. - 5. Interactive Services Model - Key Features: - Two-way communication, real-time interaction, and personalized services. Citizens can engage with government officials. It is a Government to Consumer to Government Model (G2C2G Model- See below). - Usefulness: - Customer-centric—best for personalized public services and real-time interaction. E.g., Civil consultations or service applications. - Examples: - Online consultations, interactive government portals for service requests. - Advantages: - Most interactive, enabling direct citizen-government interaction. - E-governance can also be understood through the interaction between different stakeholders such as citizens, businesses, government agencies, and employees. - - 4.4.1.1. Stakeholder-Based Models of E-Governance: G2C, G2G, G2B, and G2E -

- 4.4.2. Recent Development in India Regarding E-governance - 4.4.2.1. Digital India Mission - It was launched by the Government of India in 2015. - It aims to transform India into a digitally empowered society and knowledge economy. - This initiative focuses on three core areas: creating digital infrastructure, delivering services digitally, and promoting digital literacy. - A key component of the mission is the Common Service Centres (CSCs), which provide essential government services and digital access to citizens in rural and underserved areas. - 4.4.2.2. NeGP 2.0 or E-Kranti (Electronic Delivery of Services): - It aims to provide electronic delivery of services to citizens in various sectors such as healthcare, education, agriculture, and justice. - For instance, e-Hospital services allow online appointment bookings and access to medical records. - Enhancements in Governance: NeGP vs. NeGP 2.0 (e-Kranti) - The National e-Governance Plan (NeGP), launched in 2006, aimed to make government services accessible electronically but faced issues like lack of needs analysis, interoperability problems, and adapting to new technologies. - NeGP 2.0 (e-Kranti), introduced in March 2015 under Digital India, addresses these limitations to enhance governance. Here’s how: - Integrated Approach - Combines cloud computing, mobile platforms, and geospatial systems for better coordination and resource use. - Outcome-Oriented - Focuses on achieving real impacts rather than just completing tasks, ensuring initiatives are effective and beneficial. - Fast and Agile - Adapts quickly to technological changes, allowing for faster implementation and efficient service delivery. - NeGP 2.0 (e-Kranti) significantly advances e-governance by integrating systems, focusing on real outcomes, and being agile, thus improving governance and service delivery. - 4.4.2.3. BHIM (Bharat Interface for Money) UPI (Unified Payments Interface): - The BHIM UPI has revolutionized digital payments in India, making transactions quick, easy, and secure. - The UPI system has seen exponential growth, with billions of transactions processed monthly, reflecting the country’s shift towards a cashless economy. - 4.4.2.4. India Stack: - A set of APIs (Application Programming Interfaces) that allows governments, businesses, startups, and developers to utilize India’s digital infrastructure to deliver services. - Components like Aadhaar, eKYC, eSign, and DigiLocker form the backbone of IndiaStack. - These components streamline service delivery, reduce paperwork, and enhance accessibility for citizens. By leveraging this digital infrastructure, governments, businesses, and developers can provide faster, more reliable public services. - National Data Governance Framework Policy (NDGFP) - The NDGFP is an initiative by the Government of India aimed at creating a robust framework for managing and utilising data across various sectors. - Launched in 2022, the policy seeks to promote the ethical use of data while ensuring privacy, security, and transparency. - Key Objectives - 1. Data Accessibility: - To enhance the availability and accessibility of data for public good, ensuring that it is used effectively for governance and decision-making. - 2. Data Privacy and Security: - To establish guidelines for protecting sensitive data, ensuring compliance with privacy laws, and safeguarding citizens' information. - 3. Interoperability: - To promote interoperability among different data systems, facilitating seamless data exchange and integration across government departments and agencies. - 4. Encouraging Innovation: - To foster a data-driven ecosystem that encourages research, innovation, and the development of new technologies, enhancing service delivery and efficiency. - 5. Capacity Building: - To build institutional capacity and expertise in data management, enabling government officials and agencies to leverage data effectively. - Key Components - Data Sharing and Collaboration: - Promoting collaboration among government departments, private entities, and research institutions to create a cohesive data ecosystem. - Open Data Initiatives: - Encouraging the release of non-sensitive government data to the public to promote transparency and stimulate innovation. - Data Governance Bodies: - Establishing bodies and frameworks for overseeing data governance practices, ensuring compliance with the policy’s objectives. - Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) - Digital Public Infrastructure (DPI) refers to the foundational digital systems and platforms that support the delivery of public services and facilitate governance in a digital environment. DPI encompasses a range of technologies and frameworks designed to enhance accessibility, efficiency, and transparency in government operations. - How DPI Helps in E-Governance: - Core Digital Systems: - Identity management (e.g., Aadhaar), payment systems (e.g., UPI), and digital storage solutions (e.g., DigiLocker) form the backbone of e-governance, enabling seamless service delivery. - Interoperability Frameworks: - Standards and protocols ensure different government systems can communicate and exchange data efficiently, improving coordination and reducing redundancy. - Open Data Initiatives: - Platforms that make non-sensitive government data available to the public foster transparency, encourage innovation, and enable informed decision-making. - User-Centric Services: - Digital platforms provide easy access to government services, ensuring citizens can interact effectively with the government, enhancing user experience and satisfaction. - Advancing Data Governance in India: DGQI 2.0 - DGQI (Data Governance Quality Index) is an initiative by the Government of India to assess the data management practices of various Ministries and Departments. It aims to improve data quality, promote healthy competition, and encourage the adoption of best practices for better governance. - What is DGQI 2.0? - - DGQI 2.0 is the upgraded version of the original DGQI. It enhances the framework by: - 1. Comprehensive Evaluation: - It includes more detailed criteria for assessing data preparedness and management practices. - 2. Interoperability Focus: - Emphasizes improving data sharing and interoperability across different government departments. - 3. Actionable Insights: - Provides more actionable recommendations for improving data governance. - 4. Benchmarking Best Practices: - Encourages peer learning and the adoption of best practices from top-performing departments. - IndEA 2.0: Enhancing Digital Governance in India - IndEA 2.0 (India Enterprise Architecture) is a framework designed to improve digital governance by creating a coherent structure for government IT systems. - Key Features of IndEA 2.0: - Unified Architecture: - Provides a cohesive structure for government IT systems, ensuring all components work together seamlessly. - Interoperability: - Establishes standards and protocols that enable different government systems to communicate and share data efficiently. - Efficiency: - Optimizes resource utilization, reducing duplication of efforts and ensuring cost-effective solutions. - Enhanced Service Delivery: - Focuses on improving the quality and accessibility of government services to citizens through integrated digital platforms. - 4.4.3. Challenges - There are a large number of obstacles in implementation of e-Governance in India. These can be categorized under the following: - - Addressing these challenges requires a multi-faceted approach. - 4.4.4. Recommendations on E-governance (2nd ARC) - - Student Notes: - AI & ML for E-Governance - The incorporation of AI and ML into e-governance presents significant opportunities for revolutionizing administrative procedures and improving efficiency of public service delivery, improving citizen services, and promoting transparency in India. - - However, AI-led e-governance is not without its challenges. - - AI integration in e-governance demands secure data exchange and robust electronic infrastructure. With focused efforts and strategic investments, India can emerge as a global leader in leveraging advanced technology for public welfare. - With robust infrastructure, targeted training, and a commitment to building a dynamic workforce, India can set a global benchmark for digital governance. The key lies in ensuring that every employee, regardless of background, rank, or location, is equipped to excel in the digital age. - E-Governance is More Than ‘Use of Technology’ in Governance - Effective e-governance requires attention to both technological innovation and the redesign of administrative processes. - Use Value of Information: - Value of information lies in its capacity to empower citizens, improve transparency, and strengthen accountability mechanisms. Merely digitizing services without ensuring that information is accurate, timely, and accessible fails to harness the transformative potential of e-governance. - Multifarious Interactions: - Multifarious interactions are essential for effective e-governance. These occur not only between government and citizens but also involve civil society, private sectors, and local communities. Such engagement promotes participatory governance by enabling feedback, grievance redressal, and collaborative policymaking, thereby enhancing transparency and reducing corruption. - Institutional Reforms: - A people-first approach is fundamental to good governance, as technology alone has limited impact in rigid administrative systems. The potential of new technologies remains untapped when outdated procedures and practices are strictly enforced and misused to target officials selectively. Such controls are often exploited by corrupt actors for personal gain, hindering effectiveness of e-governance. - Ease of Governance: - The main aim of e-governance should be ‘ease of governance’ leading to ‘ease of living’ of people. When administrative procedures are easy to understand and navigate, citizens and officials can engage with digital platforms effectively, leading to higher adoption rates. Simplified governance reduces bureaucratic delays, minimizes corruption opportunities, and enhances transparency. This creates a more responsive system. - E-governance is a holistic approach that integrates technology, effective information use, and multi-stakeholder interactions to create a transparent, accountable, and inclusive governance framework. - Chapter 2 - ROLE OF CIVIL SERVICES IN A DEMOCRACY - 1. Civil Services - Civil service refers to the body of government officials who are employed in civil occupations that are neither political nor judicial. Often called the "Steel Frame of India”, they provide stability and ensure smooth transitions between governments. - Civil services are essential in public administration, significantly influencing a country’s development and progress by ensuring efficient operations. The development, upliftment and progress of society depend upon the efficient functioning of civil services. - - Civil servants operate within an impersonal system, governed by specific rules and a clear hierarchy. - - Student Notes: - 1.1. Role of Civil Services - Civil servants play a crucial role as agents of change in societal transformation by implementing policies, driving innovation, engaging with communities, managing crises, promoting social equity, fostering sustainable development, and building institutional capacity. Their key roles include: - - a. Facilitators of Democratic Processes and Good Governance: - Conduct free and fair elections and ensure smooth transfer of power at Centre and States. - Maintain uninterrupted administration during political instability (e.g., President’s Rule). - Uphold loyalty to the people, Constitution, and nation, not to any political party. - Protect unity, integrity, and constitutional values; have the courage to say “no” if laws or conventions are compromised. - b. Policy Advisors to the Government: - Serve as repositories of institutional knowledge and provide expert advice in policy-making. - Undertake research and assess financial, administrative, and legal implications of policies. - Highlight consequences of flawed policies and provide inputs for effective decision-making. - c. Policy Implementers and Administrative Leaders: - Design policies and ensure their effective implementation, including Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). - Provide leadership at senior levels to monitor and guide program execution. - Execute government programs, lay down laws, rules, and regulations. - Facilitate delivery of essential services and coordinate relief operations. - d. Maintainers of Stability and Continuity in Governance: - Act as stabilizing forces during political crises or administrative vacuums. - Rise above partisan conflicts to resolve crises and ensure smooth governance. - e. Regulators and Facilitators in a Market Economy: - Transition from service providers to facilitators and regulators in a liberalised economy. - Build institutional capacity through domain expertise to regulate markets effectively and protect public interests. - f. Leaders with Moral Authority: - Hold power, prestige, and social status; expected to lead with professionalism and ethical standards. - Set examples that influence government and private sector behavior. - Prioritize public welfare and act in a non-partisan manner. - - The competency framework for the Civil Services under Mission Karmayogi envisages to prepare civil servants for the future by making them more constructive, enabling, energetic, innovative, proactive, professional, progressive, transparent and technology enabled. - Characteristics of Indian Civil Services - Indian Civil Services possess following characteristics. However, these traits have evolved over time to ensure stability, fairness, and effectiveness in administration. - - 1.2. Evolution of Civil Services in India - - 1.3. Issues with Civil Services in India - Various scholars and committees, including the Second Administrative Reforms Commission, have highlighted criticisms of the civil services' performance as follows: - Erosion of Neutrality, Political Interference, and Subjugation to Political Executive: - Bureaucrats are increasingly exhibiting political bias in critical functions. This is both a cause and effect of increasing political interference in postings, transfers, and other administrative aspects. Senior civil servants are perceived as being subjugated by the political leadership. - E.g., despite allegations of misconduct and criticism for partisan behaviour, IPS officer Rajeev Mishra was promoted by the West Bengal government. Also, Civil servants in Delhi often find themselves caught between conflicting authorities (Centre and UT). - Lack of Specialised Expertise (Generalist vs. Specialist): - Career bureaucrats, mainly generalists, often lack the specialized technical expertise required for complex government functions, raising doubts about an IAS officer's ability to manage diverse portfolios like civil aviation, power, or defense effectively. - Early single examination rank heavily influences career trajectories, overshadowing actual job performance and expertise. - Corruption and Incompetence: - The civil service system is affected by significant corruption and incompetence at various levels, which frequently goes unpunished. - Example: "Puja Khedkar" case as instance of fraud and procedural misuse. Enforcement Directorate Assistant Director Shekhar Kumar accused of demanding a ₹2 crore bribe to halt an investigation. - Insufficient Public Engagement, Culture of Superiority and ‘Mai Baap’ Attitude (Colonial Overhang): - Lack of stringent penalties for misconduct. E.g., 2 IAS officers involved in ordering a stadium to be cleared for walking a pet dog faced no reduction in status or salary, only transfers. - Results in policies less aligned with ground realities, leading to implementation challenges and public dissatisfaction. - Demoralization and Fear of Politicization: - Moves like lateral entry threaten serving officers' hegemony and are seen as potentially compromising the neutrality and impartiality of the civil service by inducting political loyalists. - Structural Rigidity, Red Tape, Slow Changes: - Inflexible structures cause delays. E.g., During the 2018 Kerala floods, bureaucratic delays hindered a timely and effective response. - Lateral Entry Challenges - Lack of clear frameworks, concerns about transparency, potential friction with career bureaucrats, and risk of politicisation of bureaucracy (lateral entry may potentially compromise the neutrality and impartiality of the civil service by inducting political loyalists). - 1.3.1. Recent Issues Related to Civil Services - Social Media Popularity of Civil Servants and the Price of Lost Anonymity - The traditional Westminster model of bureaucracy, which India largely adopted, emphasizes the principle of civil service anonymity. A growing trend of civil servants becoming public figures, actively engaging with media and social media, is reshaping perceptions and potentially impacting the core tenets of governance. - Reasons - - Impact - - Use of Social Media in Innovative Service Delivery & Governance - Many officers use Twitter, Facebook, and other platforms to reach out to people and hear their problems. Social media helps officers for rapid grievance redressal, spreading public awareness, enhancing transparency, promoting community engagement, and sharing real-time updates on government schemes and services. - 1. Prashant Nair, IAS Officer (Kozhikode, Kerala): - Used Facebook to mobilize community efforts for cleaning a 14-acre pond. - 2. Awanish Sharan, IAS Officer (Chhattisgarh): - Introduced "Sangi Express" bike ambulances for remote tribal areas. Social media played a crucial role in spreading awareness. - As individual officers increasingly become public figures through media exposure and social platforms, the traditional institutional voice through official spokepersons risks being overshadowed by personal opinions and individual identities. This shift can undermine the collective neutrality and impartiality that civil services are meant to uphold. - Transfer as a Tool for Political Control of Bureaucracy - Despite recruitment through exams and some protections, civil servants remain vulnerable to arbitrary transfers. Politicians’ use of transfers to control bureaucracy undermines bureaucratic autonomy, fosters corruption, and weakens effective governance. - - Politicians leverage civil servants’ strong preference to be posted near their hometowns or in urban, desirable locations. - The fear of being transferred to less desirable or remote postings is used as a threat to compel civil servants to comply with political demands. Thus, officers and District Collectors often remain aligned with local Ministers to retain their positions for extended periods. - For example, a 2024 working paper “Bureaucrat Assignments as Instruments of Political Control: Evidence from Land Administration Officials in India” pointed out that Politicians pressure tehsildars to facilitate land conversions benefiting real-estate developers. Developers in turn, provide financial kickbacks to politicians in return. - Role of Civil Services Board (CSBs) - - Supreme Court in T.S.R. Subramanian case directed states to constitute CSBs to act as final authority to ensure fair postings for all officers. CSB is made up of high-ranking service members who are professionals in their domains, with the cabinet secretary at the national level and the chief secretary at the state level, who serve as the state government’s guide and advisor. - T.S.R. Subramanian Case Judgement - Establish CSB to guide on transfers, postings, and disciplinary actions. - Assurance of minimum tenure for civil servants to ensure efficiency. - Government must provide justifications when choosing to dissent from the CSB’s recommendation. - Civil servants to respond only to recorded instructions, except in exceptional circumstances, to prevent favoritism and corruption. Oral and verbal instructions, if not recorded, goes against spirit of RTI Act. - Issues: - - Government’s Resistance to Consult CSBs: - Recommendations can be amended, modified, or rejected by governments. Government contend that the IAS Cadre Rules, 1954 (2014 & 2016 amendment) don’t mandate that all transfers and postings of IAS officers shall be only on the recommendations of the Civil Services Board. Even as per the Rules, the recommendations of the Civil Services Board may be required only if the IAS officer is transferred prematurely before completion of the period of service in a post. - Using Covert Methods: - If the political leadership cannot directly transfer an officer who resists undue influence, they may resort to implicating the officer in false cases to undermine and remove them, highlighting how restricting political control through bodies like the Civil Services Board can sometimes lead to more covert and damaging tactics. - Conflict of Interests Due to Bureaucrats in Boards: - This may - To maintain a harmonious balance between the political and permanent executive, the autonomy of career bureaucrats is essential. - Why Public Perception of Civil Services Matter? - A positive public perception of a transparent, accountable, and committed civil service is the bedrock upon which citizens' trust in governance is built and sustained. It fosters a virtuous cycle that strengthens democratic institutions and processes. - Enhances Legitimacy and Trust: - A fair and effective civil service boosts government legitimacy by inspiring public confidence through transparent recruitment and grievance redressal. - Promotes Accountability and Reduces Corruption: - Accountability and transparency deter corruption; tools like the RTI Act and CVC strengthen integrity and public vigilance. - Improves Policy Implementation: - Competent and committed civil servants encourage citizen cooperation, leading to better outcomes in programs requiring citizens cooperation. E.g., Swachh Bharat Abhiyaan. - - Strengthens Democratic Institutions: - Neutral and impartial civil servants uphold the Rule of Law and democratic stability, ensuring governance continuity despite political changes. - Fosters Citizen Engagement: - Positive perception motivates active citizen participation in governance, enhancing responsive and collaborative governance. - E.g., LG Mulaqat Scheme of LG Manoj Kumar Sinha in J&K. - - Serves as Role Model: - Civil servants exemplifying integrity and professionalism cultivate civic virtues and ethical conduct in citizenry. - E.g., former Tamil Nadu DGP V.R. Lakshminarayanan was known for his political nonpartisanship, integrity and efficiency. - Ensures Stability and Continuity: - The permanent civil service framework guarantees administrative stability and sustained development beyond electoral cycles. - - In essence, a civil service that is, and is perceived to be, transparent, accountable, and committed is a vital pillar supporting the edifice of a thriving democracy. It transforms governance from a mere exercise of authority into a partnership between the state and its citizens. - 2. Civil Services Reforms in India - Civil Service Reform is a deliberate effort by the government to enhance its ability to implement policies efficiently and effectively. The main components of Civil Service Reform should pertain to the following: - - Transforming Civil Service Mindset - A paradigm shift in the nature of the civil service and its servants is necessary to address the emerging demands and changes in society and the economy.
