- 1.4. Types of Pressure Groups - Pressure groups can be categorized based on their nature, structure, and objectives. The main types of pressure groups include: - 1. Institutional Interest Groups: - These are associations of people who are part of government organization. They typically raise their concerns and protest through constitutional means. - Examples: - IAS Association, IPS Association, Government Banks. - 2. Associational Interest Groups: - These groups are specialized and pursue limited, specific goals. - They represent the interests of particular sectors or professions, including trade unions, organizations of businessmen and industrialists, students, and farmers. - Examples: - FICCI (Federation of Indian Chambers of Commerce & Industry) - ASSOCHAM (Associated Chambers of Commerce and Industry of India) - AITUC (All India Trade Union Congress) - Bar Council of India (BCI) - National Students Union of India (NSUI) - 3. Non-Associational Interest Groups: - These are informal, community-based pressure groups often formed on the basis of caste, region, religion, language, or ethnicity. - They may not have a formal structure but wield significant influence due to their large and cohesive memberships. - Examples: - RSS (Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh) - Karni Sena - VHP (Vishva Hindu Parishad) - 2. Characteristics of the Indian Pressure Groups (PGs) - Indian pressure groups exhibit several distinctive characteristics that reflect the nation's diverse and dynamic socio-political landscape. These characteristics include: - Presence of Traditional Social Structures: - Many Indian pressure groups are based on traditional social structures such as caste and religion. - Emergence Due to Resource Demands: - Many pressure groups in India have emerged because different sections of society are competing for limited resources. As resources are scarce, these groups form to advocate for their specific interests and ensure their demands are heard. - Alternative to Political Parties: - Pressure groups provide an alternative to political parties by representing people who may not be adequately represented in the formal political process. They offer a platform for diverse voices and interests. - Patronage of Political Parties: - Many associational pressure groups operate under the patronage of political parties, often acting as extensions of these parties to mobilize support and influence policy. Example: ABVP (Akhil Bharatiya Vidyarthi Parishad) is associated with the Bharatiya Janata Party (BJP). - Influence of Foreign Lobbies: - Foreign lobbies and international organizations have a considerable influence on Indian politics, advocating for policies that align with their interests and objectives. - Representation of Changing Societal Consciousness: - Indian pressure groups reflect the evolving consciousness of society, addressing contemporary issues and advocating for social change. - Examples: - India Against Corruption (anti-corruption movement) - Safai Karmachari Andolan (movement for the rights of manual scavengers) - PUCL (People's Union for Civil Liberties) - ADR (Association for Democratic Reforms) - Presence of Anomic Interest Groups: - Anomic interest groups are spontaneous pressure groups that arise in response to specific frustrations or issues. They are characterized by their collective and often rapid response to particular grievances. - Examples: - Naxalite Groups (left-wing extremist groups) - JKLF (Jammu and Kashmir Liberation Front) - ULFA (United Liberation Front of Assam) - Case Studies- Anomic Pressure Groups: - In 2024, Bangladesh saw massive student protests, initially sparked by demands to reform the government job reservation system. The movement quickly escalated into widespread unrest, challenging Prime Minister Sheikh Hasina's rule. The protests were spontaneous and lacked formal leadership, fitting the characteristics of an anomic pressure group. Violent crackdowns intensified the situation, resulting in significant public support for the protesters. Ultimately the movement led to PM Sheikh Hasina's resignation, marking a profound impact on the nation's political landscape. - In May 2025, the group Palestine Action vandalized a Jewish-owned business in London, alleging ties to Israeli arms manufacturer Elbit Systems. - Impact of Social Media and Digital Platforms on Pressure Groups in India: - Social media and digital platforms have revolutionized the functioning and effectiveness of pressure groups in India, enhancing their ability to influence public policy and mobilize support. - Positive Impacts - Increased Reach and Accessibility: - Platforms like Facebook, Twitter, and Instagram enable pressure groups to reach a wider audience, including marginalized communities, at a lower cost. - Enhanced Mobilization and Engagement: - Real-time mobilization through WhatsApp and Telegram increases participation in protests and rallies. Interactive engagement through social media fosters a sense of community and active public involvement. - Greater Visibility and Awareness: - Hashtags and viral campaigns, such as #MeToo and #JusticeForNirbhaya, draw national and international attention. Continuous media coverage keeps issues in the public eye. - Transparency and Accountability: - Real-time documentation of events and public scrutiny ensure government accountability. - Collaborative Networks: - Digital platforms facilitate global alliances, resource sharing, and crowdfunding for campaigns. - Negative Impacts - Misinformation and Fake News: - The spread of misinformation can undermine the credibility of pressure groups. - Surveillance and Censorship: - Increased surveillance and potential censorship by authorities pose risks to activists. - Echo Chambers and Polarization: - Social media algorithms can create echo chambers, limiting constructive dialogue. The anonymity and immediacy of social media can also lead to extreme viewpoints and polarization. - Digital Divide: - Unequal access to digital platforms can exclude important voices from the discourse. - Recent Examples: - 1. Farmers' Protests (2020-2021): - Social media helped in massive digital mobilization against farm laws. - 2. #JusticeForSSR (2020): - Social media campaigns demanding justice for actor Sushant Singh Rajput led to multiple high-level investigations. - 3. #StopAdani Movement (2021): - Environmental activists used social media to mobilize global protests against the Adani coal project. - 4. #StudentsLivesMatter (2020-2021): - Student-led digital campaigns led to the reconsideration of exam schedules during the COVID-19 pandemic. - Social media and digital platforms have significantly enhanced the reach, visibility, and effectiveness of pressure groups in India. While they present challenges like misinformation and digital divides, their role in modern activism is indispensable for mobilizing support, raising awareness, and influencing policy decisions. - 2.1. Criticism of Indian Pressure Groups (PGs) - Indian pressure groups face several criticisms that hinder their effectiveness as agents of interest articulation: - Focus on Parochial Interests: - Unlike pressure groups in the West, Indian pressure groups are often organized around caste, religion, and ethnicity. They tend to pursue narrow, sectarian interests rather than broader socio-economic goals, limiting their impact on national policy. - Lack of Autonomous Existence: - Many Indian pressure groups act as junior partners to political parties and are often sponsored by them. This lack of independence compromises their ability to represent their constituencies impartially. - Instability Due to Lack of Commitment: - Indian pressure groups often suffer from instability because their loyalty is frequently shifting. This lack of consistent commitment weakens their influence and effectiveness. - Propagation of Extremism and Non-Constitutional Means: - Some pressure groups propagate extremist views and resort to non-constitutional means such as strikes, protests, and violence to achieve their goals. This approach can undermine the rule of law and social harmony. - Lack of Sound Civic Consciousness: - The absence of a robust civic consciousness in India hinders the development of healthy and functional pressure groups. This deficiency results in groups that are less effective in advocating for the public good. - Political Funding and Arm-Twisting: - Political funding by pressure groups enables them to exert undue influence on the government, often prioritizing their interests over larger national concerns. This practice can lead to decisions that are not in the best interest of the broader population. - Excessive Proximity to Government: - The close relationship between some pressure groups and the government can diminish the credibility of both entities in the eyes of the public. While pressure groups have the right to advocate for their interests, the government must maintain a balance between negotiation and undue influence to preserve its integrity. - 2.2. Recommendations to Reform Functioning of Pressure Groups - To enhance the effectiveness and credibility of Indian pressure groups, several measures can be undertaken: - Broaden Focus to Socio-Economic Interests: - Encourage pressure groups to address broader socio-economic issues beyond parochial interests like caste, religion, and ethnicity to contribute more effectively to national policy and development. - Ensure Autonomous Existence and Stability: - Promote the independence of pressure groups from political parties and enhance their stability through capacity-building initiatives and fostering a culture of long-term engagement and dedication to their causes. - Discourage Extremism and Unconstitutional Means: - Implement strict regulations to prevent the propagation of extremism and the use of unconstitutional means by pressure groups. - Encourage peaceful and lawful advocacy methods through awareness campaigns and legal reforms. - Enhance Civic Consciousness: - Promote civic education and public awareness to develop a sound civic consciousness among the populace. - Integrate civic education into school curricula and encourage active citizenship and community involvement. - Regulate Political Funding and Maintain Government Integrity: - Implement transparent and stringent regulations on political funding by pressure groups to prevent undue influence. - Establish clear guidelines to manage the relationship between pressure groups and the government, ensuring that legitimate advocacy does not compromise government integrity. - Promote Inclusive Dialogue and Strengthen Legal Frameworks: - Create platforms for inclusive dialogue between pressure groups, government bodies, and civil society organizations. - Strengthen legal and regulatory frameworks governing pressure groups to ensure accountability, transparency, and ethical conduct. - By addressing these challenges through comprehensive reforms and proactive measures, Indian pressure groups can become more effective, responsible, and credible agents to the democratic process and national development. - 3. Pressure Groups and Democracy - How Pressure Groups Promote Democracy? - - 1. Supplement Electoral Democracy: - Ongoing Public Engagement: - Pressure groups keep the government in touch with public opinion between elections. For example, the India Against Corruption (IAC) movement highlighted rising public sentiment against corruption. - Voice for Minorities: - They articulate concerns overlooked by political parties, such as environmental issues, civil liberties, and women's rights. - 2. Widen Political Participation: - Grassroots Activism: - Pressure groups encourage political participation, especially among young people and those disillusioned with conventional politics, through single-issue politics and decentralized organization. - 3. Ensure Competition and Debate: - Distribution of Power: - By competing against each other, pressure groups prevent any single interest from becoming dominant, thereby promoting a balanced distribution of political power. - 4. Promote Education: - Informed Electorate: - Pressure groups foster political debates and discussions, leading to a better-informed electorate and improved public policy. - How Pressure Groups Threaten Democracy? - - 1. Increase Political Inequality: - Empowering the Powerful: - Rather than dispersing power, pressure groups often empower those already in power, increasing political inequality. The most influential groups typically have significant financial resources, expertise, and institutional leverage. - 2. Exert ‘Behind the Scenes’ Influence: - Lack of Transparency: - Pressure groups often operate out of public scrutiny, exerting influence behind closed doors. Insider groups, in particular, engage with government officials away from media and public oversight, undermining democratic transparency. - 3. Exercise Non-Legitimate Power: - Lack of Accountability: - Pressure group leaders are not elected by the public, making their influence undemocratic and illegitimate. Many pressure groups lack internal democracy, with leaders often being self-appointed or elected with low member turnout. - 4. Lead to the Tyranny of the Minority: - Minority Domination: - Pressure Groups may also impose minority views on the majority. Special interest groups can push policies that benefit a small section at the expense of the broader public interest. - While pressure groups play an essential role in a democracy by representing diverse interests and facilitating political participation, they can also threaten democratic principles. The challenges highlight the need for greater scrutiny and regulation of pressure groups to ensure they contribute positively to the democratic process.
- Governance and Administration PYQs - [1995] - Q1: Which one of the following is correct in respect of the commencement of the election process in India? - Options: (a) The recommendation for election is made by the government and the notification for election is issued by the Election Commission (b) The recommendation for election is made by the Election Commission and the notification for election is issued by the Home Ministry at the Centre and Home Departments in the States (c) The recommendation for election is made by the Election Commission and the notification for election is issued by the President and Governors of the States concerned (d) Both the exercises of making a recommendation for election and that of issuing a notification in respect of it are done by the Election Commission - Answer: (c) The recommendation for election is made by the Election Commission and the notification for election is issued by the President and Governors of the States concerned - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The election process in India, governed by Articles 324 and 356 of the Constitution (1950), involves the Election Commission (EC) recommending election dates, with notifications issued by constitutional authorities (President for Lok Sabha, Governors for State Assemblies), as established post-independence to ensure free and fair elections. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (c): - The EC recommends election schedules, and the President (Lok Sabha) or Governors (State Assemblies) issue notifications under the Representation of the People Act, 1951. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Government does not make recommendations; EC is autonomous. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Home Ministry/Departments do not issue notifications; President/Governors do. - Option (d): - Incorrect—EC does not issue notifications; it recommends them. - Related Figures: - Sukumar Sen: - First Chief Election Commissioner, shaped election processes. - B.R. Ambedkar: - Framed constitutional election provisions. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on electoral processes (e.g., Q8 [1995]), Election Commission powers (e.g., Q110 [2023]), or constitutional roles (e.g., Q34 [2024]). - Key Takeaway: - The Election Commission recommends election dates, and the President/Governors issue notifications, ensuring constitutional oversight. - Q2: Council of States: - Not more than 250 members, not more than 238 representatives of States and Union Territories. - House of the People: - Not more than 552 members, not more than 530 representatives of states plus not more than 2 nominated Anglo-Indians plus X. - Which one of the following will fit in the place marked 'X'? - Options: (a) Ministers who are not members of Parliament but who have to get themselves elected to either House of Parliament within six months after assuming office (b) Not more than 20 nominated members (c) Not more than 20 representatives of Union Territories (d) The Attorney General who has the right to speak and take part in the proceedings of either House of the Parliament - Answer: (c) Not more than 20 representatives of Union Territories - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The composition of Lok Sabha (Article 81) and Rajya Sabha (Article 80), set in 1950, includes state representatives, Anglo-Indian nominations (Article 331), and Union Territory (UT) representatives, reflecting India’s federal structure post-independence. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (c): - Article 81(1)(b) allocates up to 20 seats for UT representatives in Lok Sabha. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Ministers’ election requirement (Article 75(5)) is unrelated to Lok Sabha composition. - Option (b): - Incorrect—No provision for 20 nominated members; only 2 Anglo-Indian nominations. - Option (d): - Incorrect—Attorney General (Article 76) has speaking rights, not membership. - Related Figures: - B.R. A large number of candidates can split votes, causing even winners to lose deposits, a common issue in multi-candidate elections post-1950s. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (d): - A large number of candidates splits votes, reducing the winner’s share below one-sixth, leading to deposit loss. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Low polling does not directly cause deposit loss. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Multi-member constituencies were abolished post-1961. - Option (c): - Incorrect—Margin of victory is irrelevant to deposit loss. - Related Figures: - Sukumar Sen: - Established deposit rules as first CEC. - T.N. The joint sitting of the two Houses of the Parliament in India is sanctioned under Article 108 of the Constitution 2. The first joint sitting of Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha was held in the year 1961 3. The second joint sitting of the two Houses of Indian Parliament was held to pass the Banking Service Commission (Repeal) Bill - Options: (a) 1 & 2 (b) 2 & 3 (c) 1 & 3 (d) 1, 2 & 3 - Answer: (d) 1, 2 & 3 - Analysis: - Constitutional and Legal Context: - Article 108 of the Constitution provides for a joint sitting of both Houses of Parliament to resolve a legislative deadlock when: - A Bill is rejected by the other House, - Or the Houses disagree on amendments, - Or six months have elapsed without agreement. - The President summons the joint sitting, and it is presided over by the Speaker of the Lok Sabha. - Historical Joint Sittings in India: - First Joint Sitting – 1961: - The first joint sitting was held in May 1961 to pass the Dowry Prohibition Bill, 1959. - The Bill had faced opposition and delay in the Rajya Sabha, necessitating a joint session. - Second Joint Sitting – 1978: - The second joint sitting was held in May 1978 to pass the Banking Service Commission (Repeal) Bill, 1977. - The Lok Sabha had passed the Bill, but it was rejected by the Rajya Sabha, leading to a deadlock. - This marked the second-ever joint session in independent India. - Correctness of Each Statement: - Statement 1: Article 108 — Correct. The value of the vote of an elected Member of Legislative Assembly equals (State Population / Number of Elected MLAs of the State) × 100 2. The value of the vote of an elected Member of Parliament equals to total value of the votes of all elected MLAs divided by total number of elected MPs 3. There were more than 5000 members in the latest elections - Options: (a) 1 & 2 (b) Only 2 (c) 1 & 3 (d) Only 3 - Answer: (b) Only 2 - Analysis: - Constitutional Context: - The President of India is elected by an Electoral College under Article 54 of the Constitution. - The Electoral College consists of: - Elected members of both Houses of Parliament - Elected members of the Legislative Assemblies of states and Union territories (Delhi and Puducherry) - Nominated members are excluded from the Electoral College. - Statement 2: Value of MP’s vote = Total MLA vote value ÷ Total elected MPs - Correct - As per Article 55(2)(b), the value of each elected MP’s vote is calculated by dividing the total value of all votes of elected MLAs by the total number of elected MPs. - This ensures parity between the Union and the states in Presidential elections. - Example (2022 Election): - Total value of MLA votes ≈ 5,43,200; Elected MPs = 776 - Value of each MP’s vote = 5,43,200 ÷ 776 ≈ 700 - Statement 1: MLA vote value = (State Population ÷ No. of elected MLAs) × 100 - Incorrect - The correct formula is: - (Population of the State as per 1971 Census ÷ Total number of elected MLAs) × 1/100 - However, the product is rounded down to the nearest whole number. - Additionally, only the quotient is considered, and any remainder of 50 or more adds one vote. - The answer's formula has the 100 in the numerator, which is inverted. - Statement 3: Electoral College had more than 5000 members - Incorrect - In the 2022 Presidential Election, the total number of electors was: - Elected MPs: 776 - Elected MLAs: ≈ 4,120 - Total ≈ 4,896 (less than 5000) - This is due to recent delimitation and dissolution in certain assemblies. - Why Only Option (b) is Correct: - Statement 1 is mathematically incorrect. - Statement 3 is factually inaccurate for the latest election. - Only Statement 2 is factually and legally accurate. - Related Constitutional Articles: - Article 54: Election of President - Article 55: Manner of election of President - Presidential and Vice-Presidential Elections Act, 1952 - Broader Connection: - Important for topics on Centre-State relations, Election mechanisms, and parliamentary democracy. - Also relevant for comparative analysis with Vice President’s election, which follows a different method. - Key Takeaway: - The value of an MP’s vote is derived from a nationally balanced formula under Article 55. - Misunderstanding vote value formulas or elector totals can lead to incorrect interpretations in high-stakes examinations. - Q40: Consider the following statements: - Statements: 1. The Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs nominates Members of Parliament on Committees, Councils, Boards and Commissions etc. set up by the Government of India in the various ministries - Options: (a) 1 & 2 (b) 2 & 3 (c) 1 & 3 (d) 1, 2 & 3 - Answer: (a) 1 & 2 - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The Parliamentary Committee system, a British legacy, was formalized in India after the adoption of the Constitution. The Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs, formed in 1949, plays a crucial role in legislative coordination. - Statement 1: Rajya Sabha in PAC & CPU; Estimates only Lok Sabha - True - As per the Rules of Procedure: - Public Accounts Committee (PAC) and Committee on Public Undertakings (CPU) include members from both Lok Sabha and Rajya Sabha. - Estimates Committee is composed entirely of Lok Sabha members (30 total). Rajya Sabha members are not part of it. - Statement 2: Ministry works under Cabinet Committee on Parliamentary Affairs - True - The Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs assists in: - Planning legislative business - Coordination between both Houses - Managing parliamentary schedule and responses - It operates under the Cabinet Committee on Parliamentary Affairs, chaired by the Home Minister. - Statement 3: Ministry nominates MPs to committees in other ministries - False / Not Entirely Accurate - This is partially true but misleading: - The Ministry facilitates nominations for parliamentary committees and sometimes coordinates with other ministries, but nomination to statutory bodies, councils, boards, and commissions is primarily done by the respective ministries, not independently by the Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs. - No express rule or provision gives it exclusive or direct power to nominate MPs to every government body across ministries. - Why Only Option (a) is Correct: - Statement 1 is supported by Parliamentary Rules of Procedure. - Statement 2 is administratively verified through government documentation. - Statement 3 is overstated and factually imprecise. - Related Figures: - G.V. Mavalankar: - First Speaker of Lok Sabha, helped institutionalize the committee system. - B.R. Ambedkar: - Framed parliamentary functioning guidelines during Constituent Assembly. - Broader Connection: - Links to topics such as: - Parliamentary Committees (GS II) - Governance and Ministry structures - Legislative Procedures in Indian polity - Key Takeaway: - Rajya Sabha participates in PAC and CPU, but not in the Estimates Committee. - The Ministry of Parliamentary Affairs works under the Cabinet Committee but does not independently nominate MPs to all government boards, hence only Statements 1 and 2 are correct. - Q41: Survey of India is under the ministry of: - Options: (a) Defence (b) Environment and Forests (c) Home Affairs (d) Science and Technology - Answer: (d) Science and Technology - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The Survey of India, established in 1767, was placed under the Ministry of Science and Technology post-independence to manage mapping and geospatial data, critical for development and planning. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (d): - Survey of India operates under the Department of Science and Technology for surveying and mapping. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Defence handles military, not surveys. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Environment and Forests is unrelated. - Option (c): - Incorrect—Home Affairs manages internal security. - Related Figures: - George Everest: - Early Surveyor General, shaped Survey of India. - Kapil Sibal: - Oversaw Science and Technology Ministry. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on government institutions (e.g., Q58 [2022]), administrative structure (e.g., Q34 [2024]), or science policy (e.g., Q85 [2015]). - Key Takeaway: - The Survey of India is under the Ministry of Science and Technology for mapping and geospatial functions. - [2004] - Q42: With reference to Indian Parliament, which one of the following is not correct? Advani: - Supported linguistic inclusion debates. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on constitutional amendments (e.g., Q117 [2019]), linguistic diversity (e.g., Q34 [2024]), or Eighth Schedule (e.g., Q36 [2003]). - Key Takeaway: - The 71st Amendment (1992) added Konkani, Manipuri, and Nepali to the Eighth Schedule, but not Maithili. - Q149: Which one of the following statements is correct as per the Constitution of India? - Options: (a) Inter-State trade and commerce is a State subject under the State List (b) Inter-State migration is a State subject under the State List (c) Inter-State quarantine is a Union subject under the Union List (d) Corporation tax is a State subject under the State List - Answer: (c) Inter-State quarantine is a Union subject under the Union List - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The Seventh Schedule delineates Union and State powers. Inter-State quarantine (Union List, Entry 81) ensures national coordination, as seen during the 2020 pandemic. Inter-State trade (Union List, Entry 42), migration (not explicitly listed, but Union-controlled via citizenship laws), and corporation tax (Union List, Entry 82) are Union subjects, reflecting India’s centralized federalism post-1950. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (c): - True—Inter-State quarantine is under Union List, Entry 81. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Inter-State trade is under Union List, Entry 42. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Migration is not a State subject; Union controls via Article 6. - Option (d): - Incorrect—Corporation tax is under Union List, Entry 82. - Related Figures: - B.R. Ambedkar: - Designed Seventh Schedule. - T.T. Krishnamachari: - Shaped fiscal federalism. - Nirmala Sitharaman: - Managed Union tax policies. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on federalism (e.g., Q100 [2017]), Seventh Schedule (e.g., Q139 [2023]), or Union powers (e.g., Q34 [2024]). - Key Takeaway: - Inter-State quarantine is a Union subject; trade, migration, and corporation tax are not State subjects. - Q150: Under which of the following Articles of the Constitution of India, has the Supreme Court of India placed the Right to Privacy? - Options: (a) Article 15 (b) Article 16 (c) Article 19 (d) Article 21 - Answer: (d) Article 21 - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The Right to Privacy was recognized as a fundamental right under Article 21 (right to life) in Justice K.S. Puttaswamy v. Union of India (2017), overturning earlier rulings (e.g., Kharak Singh, 1963). This expanded Article 21’s scope, aligning with global privacy norms and impacting laws like Aadhaar. - Correct Option Explained: - Option (d): - True—Supreme Court placed privacy under Article 21 in 2017. - Why Not Others?: - Option (a): - Incorrect—Article 15 prohibits discrimination, not privacy. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Article 16 deals with public employment equality. - Option (c): - Incorrect—Article 19 covers freedoms, not explicitly privacy. - Related Figures: - Justice K.S. Puttaswamy: - Petitioner in 2017 case. - B.R. Ambedkar: - Shaped Article 21. - Justice D.Y. Chandrachud: - Authored Puttaswamy judgment. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on Fundamental Rights (e.g., Q108 [2017]), judicial review (e.g., Q129 [2021]), or privacy (e.g., Q34 [2024]). - Key Takeaway: - The Right to Privacy is a fundamental right under Article 21, per the 2017 Puttaswamy judgment. - Q151: Which of the following statements about the Ethics Committee in the Lok Sabha are correct? - Statements: 1. Initially it was an ad-hoc Committee 2. Only a member of the Lok Sabha can make a complaint relating to unethical conduct of a member of the Lok Sabha 3. This Committee cannot take up any matter which is sub-judice - Options: (a) 1 and 2 only (b) 2 and 3 only (c) 1 and 3 only (d) 1, 2 and 3 - Answer: (c) 1 and 3 only - Analysis: - Historical Context: - The Lok Sabha Ethics Committee, initially ad hoc (1997), was made permanent in 2000 to address MP misconduct, as seen in cases like the 2005 cash-for-query scandal. It avoids sub-judice matters to respect judicial processes, but complaints can come from non-MPs (e.g., public petitions), per Lok Sabha Rules. - Correct Options Explained: - Statement 1: - True—Ethics Committee was ad hoc in 1997, permanent from 2000. - Statement 3: - True—Committee avoids sub-judice matters, per parliamentary norms. - Why Not Others?: - Statement 2: - False—Complaints can be made by non-MPs (e.g., citizens), per Lok Sabha procedures. - Option (a): - Incorrect—Statement 2 is false. - Option (b): - Incorrect—Statement 1 is true. - Option (d): - Incorrect—Statement 2 is false. - Related Figures: - G.V. Mavalankar: - Set early parliamentary ethics norms. - Somnath Chatterjee: - Oversaw Ethics Committee institutionalization. - B.R. Ambedkar: - Shaped parliamentary framework. - Broader Connection: - Links to questions on parliamentary committees (e.g., Q111 [2018]), ethics (e.g., Q118 [2019]), or Lok Sabha procedures (e.g., Q104 [2017]). - Key Takeaway: - The Ethics Committee was initially ad hoc, avoids sub-judice matters, but complaints are not restricted to Lok Sabha members.
